Title: D3Tree: Learning to Prune Block-Diffusion Draft Trees for Efficient Verification

  • Authors: Hong Nie, Zhangchi Gao, Jun Yu, Bo Wang, Jianqing Sun, Shengping Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11371
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11371
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11371]] D3Tree: Learning to Prune Block-Diffusion Draft Trees for Efficient Verification(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11371)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Block-diffusion drafters propose a whole block of tokens in one forward pass, and draft trees built from their position-wise marginals increase the expected accepted length of speculative decoding. Every tree node occupies a query row in target verification, so under concurrent serving low-yield branches compete with other requests for verification capacity. Existing tree methods select verified nodes by draft probability under a fixed or cost-selected per-request budget, and thus always retain a prefix of the drafter's ranking. We present D3Tree, which learns which nodes of a block-diffusion draft tree are worth verifying. A tree-size head with 2H+2 parameters predicts each candidate's conditional survival from the draft state, the candidate embedding, and its draft log-probability. Thresholding the product of these scores along parent chains yields an ancestor-closed subtree whose size adapts to each request and decoding round. The head is trained on a frozen drafter with conditional labels on the full candidate tree; an optional joint mode also steers the drafter toward the retained reference path. At serving time, D3Tree verifies whole trees under light load and, under heavy load, allocates one batch-wide node budget by predicted survival, sized to the target's matrix-multiplication tiles. Retained trees share one ancestor-masked target pass, while construction, pruning, and scheduling run on the GPU and overlap with the host. Across six vision-language verifiers and 17 text and multimodal benchmarks, D3Tree is fastest in 70 of 72 load settings, with 3.2-4.0x speedup over autoregressive decoding for one request and 1.3-1.8x at 64 concurrent requests, where fixed-budget trees fall below autoregressive speed. Under a time-varying arrival trace, it has the lowest mean end-to-end latency in every arrival phase.

We test the sparse code on two applications, discovering land-cover sub-concepts and detecting out-of-distribution pixels by area-of-applicability estimation. Both indicate how sparse features could be used, but neither is yet reliable enough for practical use. Together, these results are a step toward Earth observation models whose features can be inspected.

  • Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) present significant challenges to source tracing of generated code, as LLM rewriting can substantially alter the syntactic and structural characteristics of watermarked code. RoCoMark is a multi-round minimax game between the watermark embedder and LLM rewriter. Finally, an adaptive attack-evolution mechanism based on Reflexion and short- and long-term memory pools is applied, enabling continuous interaction between the watermark embedder and evolving rewriting strategies. This iterative attacker-defender process progressively improves the robustness of the watermark against diverse and previously unseen LLM rewriting behaviors. Extensive experiments on four typical benchmarks, under attacks generated by five LLMs, show the effectiveness of RoCoMark. RoCoMark achieves an average detection AUROC of 74.2% and a Rewrite BitACC of 75.3%, while maintaining a code functionality pass rate above 99.7%.

Title: GenRM-Flow: Generators are Process-aware Reward Models in Flow Matching

  • Authors: Siming Fu, Ruizhe He, Zheming Fu, Zeyue Xue, Fengkai Liu, Yufei Jiao, Jie Huang, Xiaoxiao Ma, Xuan Yang, Lihui Luo
  • Subjects: cs.CV

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11421
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11421]] GenRM-Flow: Generators are Process-aware Reward Models in Flow Matching(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11421)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Aligning video generation models with human preference depends critically on the quality of the reward model that scores generated videos. Current video reward models, however, source their reward signal from a model's representational rather than generative capacity: they either repurpose vision-language models as pixel-space scorers, or train auxiliary scoring heads on top of frozen generator features. The generator's actual ability to predict velocities, denoise latents, and produce videos plays no role in scoring its own generations. We argue that this overlooks a structural property of offline preference learning. Any preference optimization objective that approximates an offline preference dataset mathematically targets a strictly defined KL-constrained optimal policy. By isolating the reward term from this analytical solution and mapping the exact generation log-likelihood to its flow-matching surrogate, the empirical reward emerges deterministically as the relative difference in continuous-time velocity-prediction errors. We formalize this equivalence into GenRM-Flow, a framework where a preference-tuned video generator inherently operates as a process-aware reward model directly in the noisy latent space. We further show theoretically that distinct fine-tuning objectives, whether contrastive like DPO and IPO or non-contrastive like RWR, are alternative optimization pathways toward the same optimal policy, each inducing a reward readout from the generator's velocity-prediction errors. Furthermore, the extracted process reward seamlessly substitutes external VLM scorers in FlowAWR. Operating purely on native latent outputs without auxiliary reward networks, this generative reward drives measurable improvements in downstream video generation.

Title: Coding Agents Propagate Vulnerabilities in Code Bases

  • Authors: Chenhao Sun, Niels Mündler-Sasahara, Mark Vero, Martin Vechev
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11455

  • Keywords: secure, security
  • Abstract: LLM-based coding agents are widely used to develop critical production software. Even though coding agents are known to occasionally write vulnerable code, there has not been any investigation into how code base vulnerabilities evolve over time under coding agent contributions. In this work, we close this gap by investigating whether and how vulnerabilities in existing code lead coding agents to introduce similar vulnerabilities in the code they write. We first evaluate coding agents on 96 tasks that require implementing a new backend application in a repository where other applications already exist. Second, we investigate repository-level code generation on 20 pairs of tasks in real C/C++ projects. In each task, the agent is asked to complete code critical to memory safety. We find that in both settings, insecure existing code leads to agents writing vulnerable code more often. In backend generation, insecure neighbors reduce the share of correct and secure implementations by 3.4% on average, and in C/C++ repository-level coding, the average gap between secure and insecure contexts reaches 35.4%. Prompting to follow existing code-style or to be careful about security increases, respectively decreases, how strongly agents propagate vulnerabilities, but neither stops them. When asked to explain their implementation choices, coding agents attribute them to following another code site in 76.5% of cases, even though they recognize the introduced vulnerability in 69.9% of cases. These findings show that agents use existing vulnerabilities as templates for new ones indiscriminatorily, turning each contribution into an opportunity to introduce more vulnerabilities. As such, as agents write a growing share of production code under decreasing human review, code bases can increasingly degrade in security if security vulnerabilities are not thoroughly fixed continuously.

Title: Robust and Private Swarm Learning via Zero-Knowledge Validator Admission and Consensus Aggregation

  • Authors: Marco Arazzi, Mert Cihangiroglu, Antonino Nocera
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11477
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11477
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11477]] Robust and Private Swarm Learning via Zero-Knowledge Validator Admission and Consensus Aggregation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11477)
  • Keywords: privacy, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Swarm Learning improves resilience and removes the need for a single trusted coordinator by distributing model aggregation and validation across participating nodes. However, it also delegates the validation of model updates to peers whose private data may be biased, unrepresentative, or adversarial, and existing Swarm Learning protocols generally do not attest that data, leaving decentralized validation as an attack surface. We present a robust, privacy-preserving Swarm Learning framework that makes verifiable data quality the condition for admission to the learning task. A validator joins only after a zero-knowledge attestation proves, without revealing any raw sample, that a reference model ran correctly on its private data. The attested outputs also yield a label-free class profile, so the network assembles a collectively IID validator pool from individually skewed validators as accurately as an oracle with the true labels (total-variation distance to uniform 0.025, against 0.021 for the oracle and 0.21 for random selection). Admitted validators score client updates and, through a Tendermint-style Byzantine Fault Tolerant consensus, agree on a gradient mask and a quality-derived aggregation weight for each submission, replacing dataset-size weighting with a quality-aware signal. Adapting the cut-layer idea of Split Learning, a challenge-based audit lets peers re-verify that a mask was computed on the attested data without an expensive end-to-end zero-knowledge proof. Across four benchmarks under non-IID partitions the framework matches FedAvg on clean data; under a gradient-scaling attack that collapses FedAvg to random chance, it retains 94% of clean accuracy on CIFAR-100 and over 99% on FashionMNIST, where FedAvg achieves only 2.1% and 11%. The result is a private, robust, and auditable foundation for decentralized learning.

Title: Learning through Capacity Awareness to Minimize Hospital Readmissions

  • Authors: Lydia T. Liu, Kara Schechtman, Pengyi Shi, Angela Zhou
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CY, stat.AP, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11493
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11493
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11493]] Learning through Capacity Awareness to Minimize Hospital Readmissions(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11493)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Predictive algorithms are increasingly used to guide hospital discharge decisions, yet these models can exhibit censoring bias for vulnerable patients due to insufficient historical data or past discharge decisions. When biased readmission risk predictors are used to inform discharges, the training data generated by discharge decisions may perpetuate similar biases when predictors are re-trained, leading to a vicious cycle. We extend a queueing-based Markov decision process for hospital discharge to incorporate periodically retrained readmission risk predictors. Our key insight is that cost-optimal, "capacity-aware" operational decision policies can naturally diversify the lengths of stay at which patient outcomes are observed, enabling consistent estimation of readmission risk without explicit fairness constraints. We prove that repeated retraining under discharge-up-to policies converges to unbiased risk estimates under well-specification. In synthetic experiments and calibrated simulations based on patient-level data from a partner hospital, we find that where commonly used threshold-on-risk policies can remain persistently biased, discharge-up-to policies reduce learning error over status quo decision policies (such as thresholding on risk) by 24%-25%. These improvements to estimation also mitigate some forms of bias, reducing readmission cost disparities (adjusted for baseline risk differences between demographic groups) by 38%-63%. Our results demonstrate how capacity-aware decision policies can improve both predictive performance and fairness by addressing censoring bias.

Title: EVAX: Towards a Practical, Pro-active & Adaptive Architecture for High Performance & Security

  • Authors: Samira Mirbagher Ajorpaz, Daniel Moghimi, Jeffrey Neal Collins, Gilles Pokam, Nael Abu-Ghazaleh, Dean Tullsen
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11498
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11498
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11498]] EVAX: Towards a Practical, Pro-active & Adaptive Architecture for High Performance & Security(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11498)
  • Keywords: security, protect, defense, attack, generative
  • Abstract: This paper provides an end-to-end solution to defend against known microarchitectural attacks such as speculative execution attacks, fault-injection attacks, covert and side channel attacks, and unknown or evasive versions of these attacks. Current defenses are attack specific and can have unacceptably high performance overhead. We propose an approach that reduces the overhead of state-of-art defenses by over 95%, by applying defenses only when attacks are detected. Many current proposed mitigations are not practical for deployment; for example, InvisiSpec has 27% overhead and Fencing has 74% overhead while protecting against only Spectre attacks. Other mitigations carry similar performance penalties. We reduce the overhead for InvisiSpec to 1.26% and for Fencing to 3.45% offering performance and security for not only spectre attacks but other known transient attacks as well, including the dangerous class of LVI and Rowhammer attacks, as well as covering a large set of future evasive and zero-day attacks. Critical to our approach is an accurate detector that is not fooled by evasive attacks and that can generalize to novel zero-day attacks. We use a novel Generative framework, Evasion Vaccination (EVAX) for training ML models and engineering new security-centric performance counters. EVAX significantly increases sensitivity to detect and classify attacks in time for mitigation to be deployed with low false positives (4 FPs in every 1M instructions in our experiments). Such performance enables efficient and timely mitigations, enabling the processor to automatically switch between performance and security as needed.

Title: ForeAct: Geometry-Guided Action Learning from World Representations

  • Authors: Wei Lu, Jiaxi Zhang, Yunheng Wang, Taowen Wang, Yuetong Fang, Zhenhui Zhang, Shuning Zhang, Zizhao Yuan, Yixiao Feng, Zecui Zeng, Renjing Xu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11500
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11500
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11500]] ForeAct: Geometry-Guided Action Learning from World Representations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11500)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Robotic manipulation requires representations that capture both scene geometry and its evolution during interaction. Existing approaches have largely treated these two aspects separately. World action models capture such dynamics by coupling action prediction with future video generation, but their reliance on iterative diffusion or rectified-flow inference leads to high computational cost, while their representations lack explicit spatial structure. Explicitly modeling scene geometry typically requires auxiliary modules that increase both parameter count and computational cost. Conversely, geometry-centric policies primarily recover observed spatial structure and often lack explicit supervision for anticipating future geometric changes during interaction. To address these limitations, we introduce ForeAct, which repurposes a pretrained video world model as a single-pass representation backbone for low-latency control. ForeAct directly processes the clean latent of the current observation in one forward pass, eliminating future-frame synthesis and iterative denoising while retaining the backbone's pretrained spatiotemporal priors. Rather than introducing a separate geometry backbone, ForeAct uses learnable tokens supervised by next-frame relative depth to shape the shared video representation through bidirectional self-attention. This compact interface turns the spatial knowledge already encoded in the backbone into a future-aware representation for action generation, with little parameter overhead. The lightweight geometry readout is used only during training and removed at deployment, enabling low-latency, geometry-aware control without depth input or an external geometry backbone. Without additional large-scale pretraining, ForeAct achieves 96.6% average success on LIBERO, 63.5% on SimplerEnv-Bridge, and a 74.4% success rate across 90 real-world rollouts.

Title: On the Relative Security of Prompt Injection: Mechanised Definitions, Defences and Verdicts for LLM Agents

  • Authors: Brijesh Dongol
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11506

  • Abstract: An LLM agent that reads untrusted content, such as an email or a web page, may follow instructions hidden in it. Noninterference is too strong for such agents, since a useful agent lets untrusted content influence what it does and may disclose the user's data by design, and declassification needs a release policy written for each task. We instead state security relative to a reference agent, which runs the same plan under the same runtime monitor with a model that never obeys untrusted content. An agent is relatively secure if every leak it has is a leak the reference already has. Our framework, PlanTERS, instantiates relative security, a notion from speculative execution, with a small agent language in which the attacker chooses how the model follows instructions, a composition with runtime monitors, and attacker models that differ in what they observe. We prove or refute relative security for agents with fences, budgets, approvals, loops and dual LLMs, in Isabelle/HOL.

Title: Probing Transformer Representations Layer by Layer, Module by Module

  • Authors: Ambroise Odonnat, Theo Gnassounou, Laetitia Chapel, Romain Tavenard, Ievgen Redko
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11531

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11531]] Probing Transformer Representations Layer by Layer, Module by Module(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11531)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Which representation should be probed in a transformer-based foundation model? Recent work shows that the standard practice of probing the final layer is often suboptimal, as intermediate layers can provide better features. Yet, representations are still largely studied along depth alone, treating each transformer block as an atomic unit. In this work, we analyze transformer representations layer by layer, module by module. Through more than 144,000 linear probing evaluations of vision foundation models (VFMs) and large language models (LLMs), we show that layer and module choices should be considered jointly: the best layer-module pair yields substantially larger gains over the final-layer residual stream than the best layer with the residual stream fixed. Moreover, module performance can vary substantially across layers, showing that useful representations cannot be characterized by depth alone. Building on these findings, we evaluate simple fixed probing strategies: the final-layer feedforward activation (Act@100) for vision and the second normalization layer at 60% depth (LN2@60) for language. These strategies improve over the probing baseline in a majority of evaluations and recover, on average, 42% and 86% of the gains from probing the best layer-module pair for vision and language, respectively. For VFMs, these gains increase with the transfer difficulty between pretraining and downstream data.

  • Abstract: This paper shifts the object of role engineering from human users to large language model (LLM) agents, and proposes PRISM to address the over-authorization that arises when an agent obtains full tool privileges through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). PRISM formulates whether a tool is necessary for a task as an intervenable causal proposition, and applies structured interventions in a sandbox to learn a counterfactual necessity model. It then compresses trajectories into discrete latent variables through an information bottleneck, and determines the number of roles from the knee of the rate-distortion curve.   • Authors: Pramath Girish Haritz, Chandra Sekhar Seelamantula

  • Abstract: In this work, we investigate whether attention-based attribution maps in a vision transformer provide causal explanations for diabetic retinopathy (DR) classification. We define an identity relating $c_t$ and $p_t$ that characterizes when $c_t$ fails to capture causality. Together, these findings show that the pull $p_t$ and the contribution $c_t$ form computationally efficient attribution maps for reliable causal interpretation in safety-critical medical diagnostics. Title: When Should Diffusion Language Models Reconsider Their Commitments?

  • Authors: Haoyang Zheng, Yidong Ouyang, Blake Jianhang Chen, Tian Tan, Ziqian Lin, Liqian Peng, Alec Go, Guang Lin
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11551
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11551
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11551]] When Should Diffusion Language Models Reconsider Their Commitments?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11551)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Masked Diffusion Models (MDMs) are well known for fast text generation via parallel decoding. However, independent parallel sampling can commit to inconsistent predictions, and less is known about how to selectively revise these early token commitments. In this work, we introduce $\underline{\mathrm{P}}$lanned $\underline{\mathrm{U}}$nmasking-and-$\underline{\mathrm{R}}$evision Joint $\underline{\mathrm{L}}$earning (PURL) for MDMs, a training-based framework that seamlessly unifies parallel generation with selective token revision. We derive a normalized joint transition whose likelihood factorizes into edit selection, unmask selection, and token prediction. These components are trained jointly on partially masked sequences constructed according to the model's own unmasking policy. During inference, all three decisions are supported by a single shared forward pass, enabling the model to decode masked tokens and revise noisy tokens. Across logic puzzles, mathematical reasoning, and code generation, PURL improves accuracy at matched unmask budgets, with relative gains up to 47.4% accuracy improvement and up to an $8\times$ reduction in scheduled decoding rounds. Beyond these gains, our analysis further reveals general principles of revisable decoding: token edits become more effective as context accumulates; early edits can steer unfinished trajectories, whereas later edits tend to remain localized; and effective edits therefore depend on both calibrating when to intervene and learning where and how to revise. Together, these suggest that effective parallel generation depends not on revising more tokens, but on making the right commitments revisable at the right time.

Title: SurfScaff3D: Bridging Geometric and Semantic Representations for LLM-based 3D Object Generation

  • Keywords: generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Creating high-quality 3D content has traditionally required specialist expertise and experience with complex tools. The growing ecosystem of AI-assisted creativity demands accessible and intuitive 3D generation systems. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer promising directions, yet extending their generative capabilities to 3D remains fundamentally challenging because existing 3D representations are not naturally compatible with language-based reasoning. Current approaches either depend on expensive fine-tuning or impose restrictive structural priors that sacrifice expressiveness and exclude organic shapes. To address this, we introduce the Hierarchical Patch Scaffold (HPS), a representation designed around semantically meaningful, interpretable part-level abstractions that are naturally understood by both humans and LLMs, enabling expressive, editable, and open-domain 3D generation without imposing restrictive geometric priors or expensive fine-tuning. Its hierarchical part-level semantic labels enable targeted, natural-language edits at any level of granularity without re-running generation. Further, HPS offers explicit per-patch control over surface detail, enabling more expressive geometry capable of representing arbitrary objects. Extensive experiments against state-of-the-art LLM-based 3D generation methods demonstrate strong geometric fidelity, semantic alignment, and flexible editing across diverse object categories.

Title: A Theoretical Reduction From Membership Inference to Reconstruction Attacks Against Learners

  • Authors: Amnon Levi, Yuval Dagan
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11680
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11680
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11680]] A Theoretical Reduction From Membership Inference to Reconstruction Attacks Against Learners(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11680)
  • Keywords: attack, membership infer
  • Abstract: We study reconstruction attacks, which recover training data from a learned model. In our adversarial model, the adversary designs a hard instance, drawn from a parameterized family of data distributions, and is given the parameter used to generate it. Our main contribution is a general reduction showing that, in this model, membership inference (determining whether a given datapoint was used for training) implies reconstruction: for a training set of size $n$, a membership test with recall at least $q(n)$ and false-positive rate $O(\delta^2/n^2)$ yields a reconstruction algorithm that, with probability $1 - O(\delta)$, outputs only training points, and recovers $\Omega(n \cdot q(n))$ of them in expectation. This reduction applies to any learning algorithm. For stochastic convex optimization (SCO), we make the reduction computationally efficient over exponentially large domains via structured distributions whose support is partitioned into polynomially many categories of exponentially many elements each, so that reconstruction iterates over categories rather than the full support. Combining this with an existing membership inference result for SCO, we prove that any learning algorithm achieving low excess risk over all distributions admits a polynomial-time reconstruction attack recovering $\Omega(n)$ training points, even when the underlying distribution has exponentially large support.

  • Keywords: secure
  • Abstract: We give a public key encryption scheme that is provably secure against poly-size adversaries, assuming $n^{\log^\alpha n}$ hardness of the standard planted clique conjecture, for any $\alpha \in (0,1)$, and a relatively mild hardness conjecture about noisy $k\mbox{-}\mathsf{LIN}$ over expanders that is not known to imply public-key encryption on its own. Title: Variational Approach to Optimal IPS Estimator for Multi-logger Off-Policy Evaluation

  • Authors: Joon Suk Huh, Junghoon Seo
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11712
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11712
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11712]] Variational Approach to Optimal IPS Estimator for Multi-logger Off-Policy Evaluation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11712)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: We study off-policy evaluation (OPE) in contextual bandits with data collected from multiple logging policies. Inverse propensity scoring (IPS) is a standard approach to OPE and extends naturally to the multi-logger setting. However, as highlighted by Agarwal et al. [2017], neither balanced nor weighted IPS consistently outperforms the other in this setting. We resolve this dilemma by deriving an optimal IPS estimator with sample-dependent weights that minimize variance subject to unbiasedness. Using a variational calculus approach, we obtain closed-form optimal weights, yielding an estimator that is unbiased and achieves asymptotically optimal variance within a generalized weighted-IPS estimator class. Instead of a reward model, the estimator needs only a $K\times K$ matrix and a $K$-vector of moments, where $K$ is the number of logging policies, and we quantify how much efficiency this costs relative to the semiparametric oracle. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that our estimator generally improves on existing multi-logger IPS methods. We also extend our estimator to a doubly robust form by incorporating a conditional reward estimator. We compare the resulting DR extension with the semiparametrically efficient DR estimator of Kallus et al. [2021], both theoretically and empirically. We show that the two estimators achieve the same asymptotic variance when the conditional reward estimator is consistent, while our estimator can retain semiparametric efficiency under certain forms of reward misspecification. Empirically, the proposed DR estimator often achieves lower estimation error than the existing DR baseline.

Title: ShadowBench-MCP: Measuring Shadow Invocations in MCP-Style Tool Routing

  • Authors: Eyhab Al-Masri, Wenjun Yang
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.MA, eess.SY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11720

  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: LLM agents increasingly act as routers for tools, APIs, and software-supply-chain workflows, making an execution path - rather than generated text alone - a security-relevant model output. We introduce ShadowBench-MCP, a benchmark and metric suite that separates task preservation (TPSS), operational pressure (OPSS), and route-geometric drift (RGAS), and combines them in the pre-execution SODS score. Policy-aware routing reduces these to 4.2% and 1.7%, respectively, corresponding to an 85.7% relative reduction in shadow invocations. Pre-execution signals distinguish shadow routes with ROC AUC up to 0.94. These results are early, single-router evidence of a policy-dependent routing-layer security effect, not a claim of universal vulnerability.

  • Abstract: Fine-tuning public pretrained language models with lightweight adapters such as LoRA has become the mainstream way to adapt them to downstream tasks. Existing secure fine-tuning protocols protect the samples but reveal to the data owner intermediate results of every training step, such as the hidden representations or logits of its inputs. We present VeilTune, to the best of our knowledge the first protocol that fine-tunes a pretrained language model with backpropagation entirely in secure multi-party computation (MPC). VeilTune computes only what the loss depends on, replaces generic MPC operators with training-aware nonlinear operators that stay accurate on the values that training produces, and obliviously skips the steps corrupted by truncation failures. On four few-shot tasks with RoBERTa-large, VeilTune nearly matches plaintext training and is 5.3 points more accurate on average than SecP-Tuning, the state-of-the-art secure fine-tuning protocol. On SST-2, it achieves a 3.5x end-to-end speedup and a 3.6x reduction in communication over SecP-Tuning, and 26x and 17x over full fine-tuning in MPC.

In this work, we propose Direct Trajectory Optimization (DTO), a training-free framework that performs alignment by directly optimizing the sampling trajectory of diffusion models. These results suggest that trajectory-level optimization is a promising direction for efficient and effective diffusion model alignment.

  • Abstract: As language models (LMs) are increasingly deployed in downstream applications, accurately estimating their likelihood of success on a given example is critical. We present DeltaForge, to our knowledge the first post-hoc mixture-of-experts VLA for driving, which consolidates N independently finetuned per-domain models into a single multi-domain model from their weights and a small proxy set alone. When different operators finetune the same base model, their weight updates end up sharing a lot of structure. We exploit this by decomposing all the updates together using singular value decomposition (SVD), splitting each one into a shared part (the same across all domains) and a tiny domain-specific part. The router then only needs to work with those tiny domain-specific pieces, not the full expert weights, which is what keeps the approach efficient. Then two cooperating clip-level blind routers, one over the VLM reasoning experts and one over the flow-matching dynamics experts, are trained from soft labels on the proxy dataset. Across six diverse domains spanning urban, off-road, and highway driving on cars, trucks, and all-terrain vehicles (ATVs), DeltaForge approaches the accuracy of the per-domain experts and surpasses every merging, routing, centralized, and federated baseline at close to single-model parameter and compute cost. Adding a new domain requires only projecting its weight update onto the shared basis with no end-to-end retraining needed. The resulting model also generalizes to domain combinations it was never explicitly trained on.

Title: Scaling Adaptive Prompt Injection Attacks with Agent Swarms

  • Authors: Alexey Kurakin, Vishal Karande, Alex Bie, Chongyang Shi, Shuang Song, Saeed Mahloujifar, Abdullatif Köksal, Jamie Hayes, Juliette Pluto, Yinghao Sun, Kamalika Chaudhuri

  • Keywords: security, defense, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Prompt injections have emerged as one of the top security risks faced by AI agents today. As of Fall 2026, human red-teaming may be nearing saturation, especially on the Gemini family of models. Title: Are UFOs Real? Introducing Unified Factored Organizers

  • Authors: Thomas X. Sheppard, Alon Albalak, Joel Lehman, Kenneth O. Stanley
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11862
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11862
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11862]] Are UFOs Real? Introducing Unified Factored Organizers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11862)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Implicit in much research in deep learning is the assumption that if a network achieves low loss, then it must have learned a good representation of its training data. Recent work by Kumar et al. (2025, arXiv:2505.11581) questions this assumption, arguing that networks trained with conventional gradient descent achieve low loss despite learning fractured entangled representations (FER), leaving related concepts disconnected and unrelated concepts entangled inappropriately. Kumar et al. further show that dramatically better representations are possible: a network evolved through an open-ended, human-in-the-loop process learned unified factored representations (UFR) that capture the regularities of the domain far better than a counterpart with nearly identical outputs trained with conventional gradient descent. Inspired by UFR, this paper introduces unified factored organizers (UFOs), networks trained to model regularities in the representations of other networks. A network can then be trained both to achieve low loss on its task objective and to align its representations with the model learned by the UFO. Networks trained with UFOs are shown to learn qualitatively superior representations in an image-regression domain where representations are easily interpretable. To provide quantitative evidence for the impact of UFOs on representation learning, a transformer is trained with and without UFO regularization to grok a modular arithmetic problem set. When combined with weight decay, UFOs can reduce the number of transformer training steps needed to grok by more than 10x relative to weight decay alone. Without weight decay, UFOs can reduce the number of transformer training steps needed to grok by more than 100x relative to an unregularized baseline. If UFOs are real, they suggest a new paradigm where loss functions themselves are trained to learn an effective model of good representation.

Title: Beyond Head Importance: Collective Computation in Video Transformers

  • Authors: Yi Yang, Siyuan Liu, Bingbing Nie
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11875
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11875
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11875]] Beyond Head Importance: Collective Computation in Video Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11875)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Attention heads in video Transformers vary greatly in functional importance. Understanding their contributions requires examining not only which heads are important, but also how they participate in collective computation within the network. We study this collective computation from two perspectives: the downstream use of head outputs and the composition of interactions across layers. Ablation analyses of four video Transformers show that high-importance heads identified on a single task also support other video understanding tasks and help preserve the representational structure that distinguishes different videos. Representational responses to head ablation correlate weakly with head importance after the attention module, but more strongly after the MLP in the same block. Adapting this downstream processing through MLP-only fine-tuning further improves the use of lower-importance head outputs. Meanwhile, through composition across layers, sets of heads with shorter spatiotemporal attention distances support stronger perturbation responses at distant tokens than sets with longer distances. Their joint ablation causes even greater performance loss than ablating equally sized sets of high-importance heads, revealing collective contributions not fully captured by individual importance rankings. These findings highlight collective computation as a key perspective for understanding attention head function: functional contributions depend on both how head outputs are used downstream and how local interactions compose across layers.

Title: How Many Candidates Does a One-Step World Model Need? A Random-Codebook Account of Nearest-of-m Training on Frozen DINO Tokens

  • Authors: Yong Hwi Song, Keon Myung Lee
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11887
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11887
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11887]] How Many Candidates Does a One-Step World Model Need? A Random-Codebook Account of Nearest-of-m Training on Frozen DINO Tokens(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11887)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: One-step stochastic world models avoid the mode averaging of deterministic feature predictors without the tens of network evaluations per step that diffusion and flow matching require. The simplest such objective is nearest-of-m matching (conditional implicit maximum likelihood estimation, IMLE): draw m candidate futures and pull only the one closest to the observed outcome. Recent work conjectures that a small m suffices for feature-space world models and that a strictly proper energy score should replace the hard minimum. We test both claims with one-step world models on frozen DINOv2/v3 patch tokens, using benchmarks whose futures can be enumerated. Under mode-like uncertainty, nearest-of-m with m=16 covers the enumerated futures better than 50-step flow matching (at about 1/30 of its latency), one-step flow generators and an energy-score generator at matched parameter counts, and its rollouts stay on the token manifold where flow rollouts drift. One quantity governs the choice of m: the nearest-of-m objective is the expected distortion of a random codebook of m draws from the generator. This view yields three predictions, recorded before the corresponding runs: (i) memorization of the realized outcome in contexts that never repeat, whose strength is governed by the codebook's miss distortion I(m) rather than its miss probability, and which an energy-score generator does not escape by raising m; (ii) a flattening of mode probabilities with m, matching a known optimum within 0.01; and (iii) under-dispersion on continuous high-dimensional outcomes, which bounds the method on real robot video. Per-step matching on self-rollouts cuts long-horizon error by a third on a stochastic Push-T variant; an ablation separates its manifold and coverage benefits. Planning success does not improve despite accurate risk estimates, a negative result we report with its diagnosis.

Title: Late but Valuable: Why Deadlines Change the Measured Value of LLM Reasoning

  • Authors: Sina Barazandeh, Benjamin E. Lundell, Jose Lugo-Martinez
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.DC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11910
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11910

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly plan and run experiments in automated laboratories. These experiments keep running while the model reasons and run on schedules with hard deadlines. If an LLM-generated answer arrives late, the system executes a safe fallback action instead, such as rejecting a sample. This creates a systems problem that standard serving metrics do not capture. An answer that matches the fallback adds no value, and answers that differ from the fallback may require more reasoning time. Reporting answer quality and latency separately can therefore overstate the value of reasoning. We test this on synthetic sample decisions, where the best choice is known exactly and a runtime enforces the deadline. We compare one LLM's fast mode, which answers directly, with its thinking mode, which reasons first. In a prespecified replay of 1,200 new decisions, thinking answers that missed a deadline were worth about five times as much as on-time ones. Multiplying the on-time rate by the mean answer value predicted about twice the value that thinking delivered. This prediction overstated thinking's advantage over fast mode by 0.0241 per decision, and by 0.0191 in a repeat on 300 new decisions. Both 95% confidence intervals excluded zero. In a single-GPU Qwen3-8B experiment using simultaneous request bursts, raising the number of requests from 8 to 56 reduced the share of thinking answers that met a 45 s deadline from 79.2% to 1.4%, although total output tripled. Evaluations of LLM agents for laboratories should therefore measure each answer's value and arrival time together and score the action that actually runs.

Title: Attention Is Only as Good as Its Witnesses: When Higher-Order Layers Help, and When They Do Not

  • Authors: Mostafa 'Neo' Mohsenvand
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.NE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11937
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11937
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11937]] Attention Is Only as Good as Its Witnesses: When Higher-Order Layers Help, and When They Do Not(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11937)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transformers stop learning graph search as graphs grow, and learn the card game SET only after many distinct questions, although attention can represent both. We read these failures, and the uneven record of higher-order attention, from what a layer sums over: each layer we study computes the partition function of a family of witnesses and reads it at a temperature. Two laws follow. The depth law is a known result of logic, which we state for these layers: L layers over two-step walks reach exactly 2^L - 1 steps, just as a logical statement with L nested clauses of the form "there is a vertex such that" sees exactly that far. We build a construction that reaches this far, checked in Lean, and, when vertices carry no names, no such network reaches further. The size law is the cost of reading at a temperature, which logic does not have: existence is read correctly at every size, in the worst case, if and only if inverse temperature times margin is at least the logarithm of the number of candidates. Layers built on these laws, and on two principles that we test, learn where Transformers fail. One propagator layer trained on 49-vertex graphs answers every question at every search depth on 250-vertex graphs. Given the modulus 3, exponential Newton computes the softmax of 2-simplicial attention over pairs exactly in 3^d n operations for d clocks; with six clocks it learns SET from as few deals as 2-simplicial attention and transfers from 8- to 20-card tables. On CLRS-30 without hints, a 186,000-parameter block of OR-join and power-mean channels matches (within half a point) or beats a model of at least 73 million parameters on 9 of 12 graph tasks of the official test set, and on 8 of 12 with freshly drawn test graphs. The same account explains where pair layers did not help and where parallel layers must loop.

Title: FireGate: Bounding Behaviour Change Before Training in Continual RL Fine-Tuning

  • Authors: Md Anwar Hossen, Ali Jannesari
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11979
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11979
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.11979]] FireGate: Bounding Behaviour Change Before Training in Continual RL Fine-Tuning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11979)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Fine-tuning a deployed language model on one task after another can change behaviour that should stay fixed, such as earlier skills, general chat or refusals. Today, such forgetting is found only after training, on the benchmarks someone chose to run. This work bounds, before training, how often its outputs change on chosen prompt distributions. FireGate places each task's update, such as a new LoRA, behind a gate, which is a classifier on the model's frozen lower layers that decides for each prompt whether the update is used. Where no gate fires, the model computes exactly what the old one computes. As a result, on any prompt distribution, every bounded behaviour score changes by at most the gates' firing rate there over a whole task stream. Calibrating each gate on unlabeled prompts of what must not change fixes that rate before training, with a PAC guarantee, whatever weights training produces. Forgetting is thus bounded by the gate's false-alarm rate, and its price is the gate's power: no update, gated or not, that changes as few protected outputs can change new-task outputs more often than the best test of that level tells new-task prompts from protected ones. On reinforcement-learning streams of up to five tasks, gated training keeps the earlier tasks and changes 3.3% of held-out chat responses, whereas ungated training changes 91.8%. Across seven models, the gain kept behind the gate follows its firing rate on new-task prompts, measured before training. The certificate covers the gated model as deployed, on the prompt distributions sampled for calibration, not against an adversary who searches for prompts that fire the gate.

  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in image generation and editing, yet this progress has also introduced significant security risks. Prior work has shown that adversarial prompt can induce the generation of Not-Safe-For-Work (NSFW) content, including pornographic, violent, and graphic imagery. However, such attacks are typically mitigated by prompt filters and often rely on white-box assumptions. In this paper, we propose AnyBreak, a novel jailbreak framework that constructs adversarial images via multi-scale progressive semantic alignment. During the generation process, these adversarial images steer the generation trajectory in latent space towards unsafe data manifolds, enabling cross-modal transfer of malicious concepts from the text modality to the image modality. AnyBreak operates using only clean textual prompts and adversarial images at inference time, and can jailbreak any T2I models towards any malicious concepts in a fully black-box setting. We further introduce AnyBreak-V, an enhanced variant that incorporates a compensation term to bypass post-hoc safety checkers. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AnyBreak achieves high attack success rates while preserving visual fidelity and semantic consistency with the input prompts under black-box and clean-text constraints. Our findings reveal that existing T2I defense leave critical cross-modal attack surfaces insufficiently addressed. The code is available at this https URL.

Title: Scalable Bayesian Optimization for Efficient Exploration of Moire Patterns

  • Authors: Panagiotis Krokidas, Christos Lazaridis, Vassilis Sioros, Efi-Maria Papia, Vassilios Constantoudis, George Giannakopoulos
  • Subjects: cs.LG, physics.app-ph
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12010

  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Large materials design spaces often contain rare high-performing configurations that are difficult to identify through trial-and-error exploration or purely representative machine-learning (ML) sampling. In this work, we present FruBO, a flexible Bayesian Optimization framework for accelerating the discovery of optimal configurations in computationally demanding materials design problems. As a case study, we investigate multilayer Moiré patterns generated by the superposition of six rotated hexagonal lattices, where the objective is to identify the rotational conformation that maximizes pore-size uniformity, quantified using the Jain fairness index. These results demonstrate that scalable surrogate models can substantially improve the feasibility and efficiency of BO in large nanomaterials design spaces.

Title: Robust Domain-Mixture Scheduling for Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation

  • Authors: Yankai Chen, Sida Lin, Yifan Li, Hanjun Luo, Hanrong Zhang, Zhuohan Xie, Bowei He, Wei-Chieh Huang, Meng Ding, Wei Xue, Yike Guo, Philip S. Yu, Xue Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12012
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12012
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12012]] Robust Domain-Mixture Scheduling for Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12012)
  • Keywords: protect, robust
  • Abstract: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) merges the capabilities of multiple domain-expert teachers, such as mathematical reasoning, code generation, and instruction following, into a single compact student. Since all domains share a limited training budget, how to allocate it across domains directly determines the quality of distillation. Existing methods allocate the budget by each domain's learning progress, but offer no guarantee for lagging domains. We therefore introduce distributionally robust optimization (DRO) into MOPD to prioritize the worst-performing domain. However, the per-domain training signals are estimated from a few rollouts and are inherently noisy, so directly applying DRO mistakes noise for the worst case, lets occasional spikes hijack the budget, and protects inherently hard domains rather than truly regressing ones. To address these issues, we propose RDS-MOPD, which keeps the DRO objective and redesigns its three parts: it shifts the allocation only when differences clearly exceed the noise, bounds the influence of any single anomaly, and defines the worst case as the regression from each domain's own best level, all with theoretical guarantees. Experiments show that RDS-MOPD outperforms recent competing methods and ranks first on most mathematical reasoning, code generation, and instruction-following benchmarks.

Title: FocusBranch: Combinatorial Branch-and-Bound for $\ell_0$ Neural Network Robustness Verification

  • Authors: Renwei Deng, Yang He, Linyi Li, Yuepeng Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12024
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12024
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12024]] FocusBranch: Combinatorial Branch-and-Bound for $\ell_0$ Neural Network Robustness Verification(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12024)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Verifying the local robustness of neural networks under $\ell_0$ perturbations is inherently combinatorial, as it must reason over all sparse subsets of $\epsilon$ perturbed pixels. This paper presents FocusBranch, a combinatorial branch-and-bound framework for $\ell_0$ robustness verification. The key insight is to make the $\ell_0$-ball decomposable and branchable using a node abstraction, which consists of a focus pixel set $\mathcal{S}$ and a level $l$, permitting at most $\epsilon-l$ pixels outside $\mathcal{S}$. This abstraction unifies two branching mechanisms: concrete nodes branch through upper shadows to fix additional perturbed locations, while super nodes branch through covering designs to cover many perturbation cases with fewer child nodes. FocusBranch proves node safety by concretizing linear relaxations over node-constrained $\ell_0$-balls and recursively branches on unknown nodes. Rather than relying on a fixed verification plan, it samples nodes across levels to estimate safe rates and costs, then selects a starting level and branching strategy predicted to minimize verification time. Experiments on fully connected and convolutional classifiers for MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10 show that FocusBranch verifies $\ell_0$ robustness effectively across architectures and outperforms state-of-the-art verifiers. The code is available at: this https URL.

  • Abstract: Constrained preference-conditioned MORL typically assumes full task observability, treating preferences and safety budgets as stationary task signals. We therefore formulate constrained preference-conditioned MORL as a partially observed task-specification problem, where the agent must navigate a fundamental conflict between unreliable external signals and latent interaction dynamics. Experiments on diverse multi-objective control tasks demonstrate that BECON consistently improves over state-of-the-art MORL baselines, achieving stronger robustness and constraint satisfaction under task drift.

  • Abstract: Video diffusion transformers (DiTs) generate realistic videos, but their heavy computational cost limits broader real-world applications. We show that these failures are not inherent to mixed resolution, and propose $\textbf{MPEG-Diffusion}$ to address their three root causes: (1) dual-resolution attention to mitigate the curse of mixed resolution, where each query reads the K/V of its own resolution, (2) a mixed-resolution decoder to blend the HR and LR latents before decoding and resolve distortion, and (3) a self-selected HR region using the DiT's knowledge to find the visually important region without requiring it in advance. In the user study, we demonstrate the high quality of MPEG-Diffusion, with raters preferring it over prior mixed-resolution methods in over $90\%$ of pairs and rating it close to full resolution.

Training and evaluation run the game's own compiled assembly (v0.107.1) in a headless .NET host. The host replays a run exactly from its seed and actions, so training episodes can restart from states the learner has visited, and a failure-driven curriculum allocates these restarts in proportion to where complete runs are lost. On 12,800 held-out games at Ascension 10 (A10), the released policies win 8.05% averaged over the five characters (Ironclad 15.6%, Regent 12.5%), and AlphaSpire's released networks used without search win 0.55% on a common panel. Longer A10 training raised Ironclad from 12.4% to 15.6% and Defect from 3.0% to 8.1% on these games. At Ascension 0 the released policies win 80.2%, level with the 80.3% that the tree-search agent Spire Mentor reports, while Mentor leads by 5 to 8 points from A4 upward. Run in our host on the same 2,000 A10 games, Mentor's search wins 8.8% against 8.2% for our policies, which need 21-27 ms per decision on one CPU core. GPT-6 Astra at maximum reasoning won none of 11 A10 games through our text interface. In a controlled study of 18 training runs from six random initializations, failure-driven allocation exceeds uniform restart allocation on each of three Regent initializations, by 3.6 percentage points on average, while the Silent effect is small and changes sign. Code, trained policies, an in-game mod and the records behind every table are at this https URL.

Title: StealthyPoison: Manipulating LLM Agent Behavior through Procedural Poisoning of Third-Party Skills

  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12155
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12155
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12155]] StealthyPoison: Manipulating LLM Agent Behavior through Procedural Poisoning of Third-Party Skills(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12155)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, steal
  • Abstract: LLM agents increasingly retrieve third-party skills from public registries and follow their procedures to complete user tasks. This reliance allows attackers to manipulate agent behavior by poisoning the skills. However, overtly malicious instructions can provide explicit signals for detection. We present StealthyPoison, a stealthy procedural poisoning attack that corrupts a skill's decision-critical rules to induce incorrect task outcomes while preserving its advertised functionality and normal execution. Unlike prior knowledge corruption attacks that craft poisoned content for known target question-answer pairs, StealthyPoison constructs reusable poisoned procedures from a publicly available third-party skill, without access to actual user requests or their ground-truth answers. To make the poisoned procedure generalize to unseen requests, it first synthesizes requests from the target skill and executes its clean version using a surrogate agent to obtain pseudo-references. These references and surrogate execution feedback guide the iterative refinement of payloads that alter decision-critical rules. To increase the likelihood that the poisoned skill is retrieved and selected over benign alternatives, the attack augments the poisoned skill's retrieval metadata with independently generated request descriptions. We evaluate StealthyPoison on 566 eligible target skills and 5,244 requests against more than 26,000 registry entries. It improves top-1 poisoned-skill placement by 13.6% relative to both HotFlip and GCG. Constructed using feedback from a single surrogate model, the procedural poisoning payloads transfer effectively and without modification to three held-out victim models and achieve end-to-end attack success in a Claude Code workflow powered by Opus 4.8. The defense evaluation further shows that most of these payloads evade detection by both SkillSpector and GPT-5.4-mini.

  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: Training a neural network under homomorphic encryption requires transposes, batch reductions, optimizer updates, and repeated refreshes in addition to forward matrix products. Its CUDA path keeps training and bootstrap ciphertext arithmetic on the GPU, while retaining host key generation and client operations. These are single-initialization results; exact CPU/GPU parameter agreement is verified at 100 updates, not for the full 1,000-update run. Earlier completed CPU runs support a larger-batch key-switch advantage and baseline-batch accuracy within the registered tolerance, but the baseline-batch normalized key-switch target is missed. The released ReBoot baseline does not meet the common security-estimator threshold, and its full-setting CPU time is unmeasured. We therefore distinguish measured acceleration of our implementation from conditional layout comparisons and do not claim unconditional superiority over ReBoot.

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Graph transformers can capture long-range interactions, but their quadratic attention cost limits their use on large graphs. We introduce \emph{NearFormer}, a graph transformer whose global attention is restricted to a randomized near-neighbor (NN) graph. NearFormer uses an efficient randomized construction to connect each node to a small number of near neighbors from a broader neighborhood defined by a label-independent feature or spectral geometry. For a fixed retained budget \(k\), the resulting attention support has linear size \(\mathcal{O}(|E|+nk)\). The NN graph is built once by within-bin search and then held fixed. We use these edges as sparse global-attention supports, with optional learned attention biases, while preserving the original graph for local message passing. Experiments on long-range graph benchmarks and synthetic structural tasks show that NearFormer provides a favorable accuracy--scalability trade-off relative to local message passing, dense and sparse attention.

  • Abstract: Evaluating generative models requires measuring not only sample fidelity but also how well a generated set covers the real data distribution. Recent work has therefore introduced recall-oriented metrics for distribution-level evaluation. However, existing recall metrics often reduce distributional coverage to geometry-based coverage. This simplifying assumption weakens their diagnostic value and interpretability in two ways: these metrics provide only a single aggregate score that cannot distinguish essential diversity failures such as mode dropping and mode collapse, and geometry-based coverage can provide unreliable estimates by assigning high scores to poorly covered generative distributions. We therefore introduce CADMA, a capacity-aware framework for recall-oriented evaluation and diagnosis. CADMA evaluates distributional coverage through local capacity matching between real and generated samples, separating geometry-based coverage from effective coverage. This yields multi-dimensional coverage diagnostics that quantify mode dropping and mode collapse while reducing the overly optimistic estimates produced by geometry-based metrics. Through extensive experiments on toy data, real-world datasets, and generative models, we show that CADMA provides more reliable coverage estimates and more fine-grained, interpretable diagnostic signals of diversity failures than existing metrics. Our code is available at this https URL.

Title: Public, Identifiable, and Effectively Immutable: Ethical Lessons from Research on arXiv

  • Authors: Jan Pennekamp, Johannes Lohmöller, Peter Mayer, Arjun Arunasalam
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12271
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12271

  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: Ethical decision-making is central to safe cybersecurity research. Recent studies, one of which is our own, analyzing the source files of arXiv papers bring this challenge to light - arXiv publicly distributes the source files, to the surprise of many authors. Unique aspects reveal ethical challenges and dilemmas that extend beyond established ethical frameworks, institutional processes, and community best practices. Second, we discuss the ethical issues surrounding the use of arXiv data and derive four higher-level dilemmas that researchers may face when working with similar data. Third, we derive practical guidance for ethically navigating such settings and, in doing so, distill four lessons for research on arXiv and similar public, live datasets: public availability should serve as the starting point of ethical reasoning; research should minimize the amplification it induces on exposed information; disclosure should be calibrated to the harm, confidence, and actionability of a finding; and effective immutability must be accounted for. Together, these contribute to community resources for ethically-sound cybersecurity experiments.

Title: An Explicit Below Half Threshold Bound for the Local Leakage Resilience of Shamir's Secret Sharing Scheme

  • Authors: Cheng Zhang, Xin Wang, Xiangliang Kong
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.IT
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12325
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12325
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12325]] An Explicit Below Half Threshold Bound for the Local Leakage Resilience of Shamir's Secret Sharing Scheme(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12325)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: As a fundamental cryptographic primitive, secret sharing distributes a secret among multiple parties and allows certain authorized parties to recover the secret while preventing unauthorized parties from knowing the secret. However, local leakage from each party member allows an adversary to learn partial information from each share, which is not guaranteed by traditional security. In this paper, we study bounds on the reconstruction threshold of Shamir's secret sharing scheme over prime fields against arbitrary 1-bit local leakage. We show that, for all sufficiently large $n$ and every prime $p>n$, the $(n,t)$-Shamir's scheme is $\exp(-\Omega(n))$-resilient whenever $t/n\geq 0.489$, for any choice of distinct nonzero evaluation points. Our proof refines the dual-code list recovery analysis and combines complementary Fourier estimates according to the support sizes and biases of the leakage functions, yielding an improved explicit reconstruction threshold bound.

Title: Optimal Rates for Adaptive Private $k$-PCA

  • Authors: Johanna Düngler, Amartya Sanyal
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12419
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12419
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12419]] Optimal Rates for Adaptive Private $k$-PCA(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12419)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Given $n$ i.i.d. random matrices $A_i \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$ with common expectation $\Sigma$, the goal of \emph{Differentially Private Stochastic PCA} is to identify a $k$-dimensional subspace capturing the leading variance directions of $\Sigma$, while preserving differential privacy~(DP) for each individual sample $A_i$. \citet{dungler2025iterative} introduced \kdppca, the first algorithm to simultaneously (1) achieve sample complexity $n=\widetilde O(d)$ for sub-Gaussian data, (2) adapt its privacy noise to the intrinsic randomness of the data, and (3) extend seamlessly to any target dimension $k\le d$. However, its sample complexity has a suboptimal dependence on $k$. We propose the first algorithm that achieves optimal sample complexity in both $d$ and $k$, while retaining properties (2) and (3) of \kdppca. Additionally, prior utility guarantees require a minimum sample size with exponential dependence on the eigengap. Our method removes this dependence. It also improves the dependence on spectral parameters, matching known lower bounds for the spiked covariance model. Unlike deflation-based approaches like \kdppca, our algorithm updates the full $d\times k$ subspace jointly rather than one eigenvector at a time. This non-deflation structure simplifies the algorithm, reduces the number of hyper-parameters, and improves computational efficiency, particularly when using block linear algebra libraries.

Title: Two-Phase Rapid Simulation-Based Inference with Differentiable Simulators

  • Authors: Uros Zivanovic, Rahul Srinivasan, Roberto Trotta, Andre Scaffidi
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12422
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12422
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12422]] Two-Phase Rapid Simulation-Based Inference with Differentiable Simulators(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12422)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: We introduce Rapid Simulation Based Inference (RSBI), a diffusion-based variational approach to likelihood-free Bayesian inference that achieves high sampling efficiency under large prior-to-posterior volumes. RSBI builds on advances in Schrödinger Bridge (SB) diffusion sampling to handle multi-modal posteriors, with an initial likelihood supplied by a distance measure via a differentiable simulator. The key innovation of this initial surrogate likelihood is that it gives a highly efficient proposal distribution, replacing sequential rounds of other methods with an efficient one-shot proposal. Subsequent refinement then transports the proposal to the true posterior as specified by the simulator and the original prior. The SB sampler is warm-started from the proposal, and its energy is corrected by a neural likelihood ratio that is learned only where the proposal places mass. As a variational method, RSBI does not suffer from prior leakage commonly observed in standard sequential posterior estimation methods. To improve mode coverage we optionally utilize the well-tempered meta-dynamics framework, which also encourages exploration of the prior volume. We observe strong performance on standard SBI benchmarks, particularly when the prior volume is increased relative to the ground truth posterior, achieving a performance degradation of only $\sim5\%$ on Two Moons under a highly constrained simulation budget. Additionally, we evaluate RSBI's performance for gravitational wave ring-down posterior estimation, in a real-world physics benchmark inspired by black hole spectroscopy.

Title: UCB-min: Combining Bandits Under Variance Heterogeneity (Extended Version)

  • Authors: Lilia Izri, Benoit Groz, Silviu Maniu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12427
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12427
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12427]] UCB-min: Combining Bandits Under Variance Heterogeneity (Extended Version)(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12427)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Multi-armed bandits are a natural framework for many real-world sequential decision problems, from recommender systems and clinical trials to educational test recommendation and online advertising. The performance of these algorithms depends on how they explore different arms, and exploration strategies range from variance-independent (e.g., UCB1) to variance-aware (e.g., UCB-V). In practice the reward variance of each arm is rarely known and varies widely across arms, and the reward support is often underspecified. In these cases no single rule is uniformly best: variance-independent methods are robust but loose when variances are small, while variance-aware methods are tight only when their variance estimates and the assumed support bound are reliable. Such heterogeneous, unknown variances arise in many settings, for instance when multiple objectives are scalarized into a single reward. We propose UCB-min, a per-arm adaptive meta-algorithm that selects, for each arm, the tighter of a variance-independent and a variance-aware exploration bonus, without prior knowledge of the variance regime and without reward normalization. We prove that, with UCB-V as variance-aware base, its regret is bounded by the sum of the two base bounds at the standard exploration constant, and by their per-arm minimum, up to a constant, for a larger one. We validate it on synthetic data, an educational tutoring task, and two group-recommendation benchmarks (MovieLens, Steam), showing that UCB-min consistently tracks the best base algorithm across diverse variance regimes and datasets.

We therefore construct UniHam, a large-scale Hamiltonian dataset and benchmark suite comprising 100,000+ DFT-computed complex-valued Hermitian Hamiltonians with full SOC, covering 72 elements and a wide range of crystal geometries and symmetries (spanning diverse lattice types and space-group families). Evaluating five open-weight models (0.8B to 27B) alongside generative System 2 baselines -- both single-token logprob scoring and structured JSON/CoT decoding -- across NLI (e-SNLI), adversarial syntactic shortcuts (HANS), synthetic clinical triage (N=900), prompt injection defense (Tensor Trust, N=250), and an 86-case logic-gate trigger suite yields five findings: (1) provider confidence is an exact closed-form dispersion transform (1 - H(p)/ln K on sub-10B models; normalized margin on Clef-27B) rather than empirical accuracy; (2) bundling multiple questions in one request induces directional cross-question interference (11.7% to 22.4% binary flips on Tev1-0.8B, vs. 0.1% to 0.2% on Tev1-4B); (3) System 1 models with >= 4B parameters are faster and better calibrated under syntactic shifts (ECE_w = 0.030 to 0.087) than logprob (0.144) and JSON (0.378) baselines; (4) for prompt-injection defense (N=250), >= 4B models achieve 0.988 to 0.998 AUROC with up to 95.2% recall and 0.0% false alarms at 48 to 60 ms; and (5) single-pass inference makes 512-coalition XAI interactive (1.3 to 3.2 s/input), while KernelSHAP with a logit link (S1-SHAP) mitigates softmax saturation on disjunctive OR gates (89.3% to 100.0% gate recovery vs. 57.1% to 82.1% in prob space; p_adj = 0.004).

Title: ML Models Are Not Ciphers: Power Analysis for Weight Extraction Is Harder Than You Think

  • Authors: Mohammed Qaiss, Peter Horvath, Michal Shagam, Lejla Batina, Zhuoran Liu, Eyal Ronen, Mahmood Sharif, Yuval Yarom
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12532
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12532
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12532]] ML Models Are Not Ciphers: Power Analysis for Weight Extraction Is Harder Than You Think(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12532)
  • Keywords: attack, extraction, diffusion
  • Abstract: Can power-analysis techniques reveal secret weights of machine-learning models? Multiple recent works demonstrate proof-of-concept attacks that can partially recover such weights. To that end, these works build on techniques from cryptographic engineering, treating the model as a ``cipher'' and its secret weights as keys. We question the basic assumption behind such straightforward adaptations of cryptographic attacks and highlight the challenges arising when applying past techniques to fully recover all layers of deep neural networks. Unlike past works, which extract only a few weights, typically from shallow layers, we investigate the recovery of complete networks. Moreover, we target fully trained and functional models, rather than untrained, randomly initialized networks. To focus on fundamental challenges, we assume an unrealistically strong side-channel adversary that can directly observe the output of the leakage model without any added noise. With this adversary, we show that machine-learning models differ significantly from cryptographic implementations, giving rise to unique challenges that have not been observed in the prior art. We observe two main differences between machine-learning models and ciphers. First, in contrast to the high-diffusion nonlinear integer arithmetic prevalent in cryptographic schemes, machine-learning models tend to use limited-precision floating-point arithmetic and low-diffusion linear operations. We show that these operations introduce difficulties in weight recovery, often requiring an unexpectedly large number of traces, even under ideal channel conditions. Second, we observe that, unlike ciphers, which aim to increase the data entropy, machine-learning models aim to reduce it. This reduces data variability as the algorithm progresses, hampering weight recovery in deep layers of the model. (details can be found in the paper)

Title: Language Defaults Before Content: A Causal Layer Map of Multilingual Generation

  • Authors: Shenbin Qian, Yves Scherrer
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12538
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12538
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12538]] Language Defaults Before Content: A Causal Layer Map of Multilingual Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12538)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multilingual generation requires large language models (LLMs) to determine both what to say and in what language to say it, yet these two commitments are difficult to disentangle from model outputs alone. We use translation as a controlled probe: for multi-parallel segments that a model translates well in one language direction but poorly in another, content is held fixed while language varies. Using activation patching, we write residual-stream states from successful runs into failing ones and compare content-matched states with content-independent controls, allowing us to trace content-specific and language-related signals across layers. Across three LLMs of different scales and architectures, we find a consistent three-stage pattern of multilingual generation. Intermediate layers first express a default English signal; content information becomes causally available one layer later; and the final target language is resolved only at later layers. The ordering of the English default before content also appears in a reading-comprehension task. Our sparse autoencoder analyses reveal a further dissociation: the intermediate language signal is only weakly represented by sparse features, whereas a later language distinction is clearly sparse, but resists intervention at the patched position. These results provide a mechanistic account of how language selection and content information emerge and interact during multilingual generation.

Title: GHiML: VLM-Guided Hierarchical Multi-Label Learning with Applications to Benthic Imagery

  • Authors: Isaac Xu, Devi Ayyagari, Martin Gillis, Benjamin Misiuk, Craig J. Brown, Thomas Trappenberg
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12564
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12564
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12564]] GHiML: VLM-Guided Hierarchical Multi-Label Learning with Applications to Benthic Imagery(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12564)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Vision language models (VLMs) encode substantial expert knowledge within their weights, yet their direct application to specialised classification domains such as benthic imagery remains limited. We propose Guided Hierarchical Multi-Label (GHiML) learning, a pipeline that extracts conceptual latent variables from a VLM conditioned on training data, generates sample-relevant captions grounded in those variables, and fuses the resulting textual embeddings with image embeddings via cross-attention and a per-node gated router. We also introduce the inverted HML score ($d_{\mathrm{inv}}$), a similarity score that evaluates hierarchical multi-label predictions through graph traversal distance and a cardinality penalty, providing an interpretable alternative to existing measures, which are insensitive to realistic degradations in sparse, deep hierarchies. Experiments on BenthicNet, FathomNet and iNaturalist show that the router improves macro $F_1$ over the vision-only baseline by up to 6.7 pp and $d_{\mathrm{inv}}$ by up to 21.1 pp on in-domain benthic data, with gains persisting under distribution shift. Our results support the hypothesis that VLMs contain exploitable specialised knowledge that, when appropriately elicited, can augment classification in domains where direct visual learning is insufficient.

  • Abstract: Access-control policies are written in natural language but enforced as formal models, and translating them into NIST Next Generation Access Control (NGAC) graphs remains unsolved: prior systems cover only subsets of the NGAC model, and none verify the graphs they extract. Reported accuracy is wording- and benchmark-specific, adversarial input is outside the verifier's guarantees, and the intended deployment is extraction plus structural verification feeding expert review.
