Title: Fairness in Explainable AI Narratives: Decomposing Social Disparities in LLM-Generated Explanations

  • Authors: Wannes Van den Bulck, Sofie Goethals, David Martens
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12592
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12592
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12592]] Fairness in Explainable AI Narratives: Decomposing Social Disparities in LLM-Generated Explanations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12592)
  • Keywords: protect, fair, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to translate technical explainable AI (XAI) artefacts such as SHAP into explanatory narratives (XAINs) for lay users. In high-risk domains, these explanations are often the main resource through which individuals can understand and contest adverse decisions. Yet LLMs are known to reproduce social bias, even from proxy variables alone, and fairness in XAINs remains unstudied. Because XAINs are bound to a ground-truth artefact, fairness concerns not only what a narrative says and how, but also whether it is equally faithful across social groups. We introduce fairness in XAINs as a new field of research and propose a framework that generates narratives under three conditions (protected attribute disclosed, withheld, or counterfactually swapped), allowing five comparisons that pinpoint the cause of disparities. We find significant disparities across all datasets, metric families and models, arising in two ways. Feature coverage and sentiment disparities stem from the protected attribute itself: once it is disclosed, LLMs give less explanation to, or frame more negatively the narratives of, groups that have historically been discriminated against or are stereotypically associated with worse outcomes. A more faithful model removes the faithfulness disparities, and withholding protected attributes mostly removes the others, but neither suffices on its own. Fairness in XAINs should therefore be audited separately from the predictive model and its XAI artefact.

To create an information advantage, Bob adds artificial noise (AN) to his challenge, and Alice adds spatial AN while forwarding the received mixture. With equal antenna numbers at all nodes, we show scenarios where the proposed scheme has lower worst-case missed detection than specified one-way tests with/without AN.

Title: KiteNorm: Variance Regularisation for Stable and Scalable Post-LN Transformers

  • Authors: Leon A. Trochelmann, Sajad Movahedi, Shiwei Liu, Antonio Orvieto
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12729
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12729
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12729]] KiteNorm: Variance Regularisation for Stable and Scalable Post-LN Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12729)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Post-LayerNorm Transformers have seen limited practical adoption due to enduring difficulties in scaling them to large depths. While prior research has focused on stabilising Post-LayerNorm by improving conditioning at initialisation, stability often deteriorates when models are trained with large learning rates, forcing additional compromises. In the decoder-only settings we study, existing Post-LayerNorm methods fail to outperform strong Pre-LayerNorm baselines. We propose KiteNorm, a post-norm training method designed to break this trend by constraining hidden-state variance. This approach is motivated by a bound on the LayerNorm Jacobian suggesting that variance growth contributes to gradient vanishing, alongside empirical evidence linking it to rank collapse. KiteNorm combines residual scaling and variance regularisation to maintain stability throughout training, supported by scalarised double-normalisation that provides separately learnable scales for the skip and residual branches. Across decoder-only Transformers up to 1.3B parameters, KiteNorm improves training stability and outperforms leading baselines at their optimal learning rates, with scaling laws favouring KiteNorm across depth, width, batch size, and training budget. Ablations show that each component is necessary for the full stability and performance gains.

Title: DeepChronos: Temporal Neuro-Symbolic Reasoning for Multi-Step Attack Detection

  • Authors: Sofia Burkow, Andrea Agiollo, Enkeleda Bardhi, Fernando Kuipers, Kaitai Liang, Georgios Smaragdakis
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.NI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12760
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12760

  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: Modern cyber threats commonly unfold as multi-step attacks comprising temporally and causally related phases. Current defenses commonly follow a detect first and correlate later paradigm. Intrusion detection systems classify events independently, while downstream systems reconstruct attack progression from the resulting alerts or audit records. In this context, learning-based detectors learn attack-step dependencies implicitly as statistical regularities in the data and cannot guarantee that their predictions follow the real attack progression. Conversely, correlation systems represent attack relationships explicitly, but apply this knowledge only after the underlying attack observations have been detected. We present DEEPCHRONOS, a neuro-symbolic network intrusion detection system that shifts the paradigm from detect first and correlate later to detection through logical progression. DEEPCHRONOS combines learned flow-level evidence with explicit attack-phase rules, temporal prerequisites, and previously inferred phases, allowing the evolving attack history to inform and constrain each detection decision. Evaluations on DARPA 2000 and AIT-LDS V2 show that DEEPCHRONOS improves macro F1 over purely neural baselines by up to 11.3%, eliminates causal violations under strict temporal constraints, and maintains millisecond-scale inference latency. These results demonstrate that integrating explicit attack progression knowledge into detection produces more accurate and temporally coherent multi-step attack predictions.

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12777

  • Keywords: protect, transformer
  • Abstract: Prompt-based continual learning adapts a frozen pre-trained vision transformer by tuning a small set of prompt tokens. Recently, one-prompt methods which share one single prompt across all tasks make this paradigm maximally parameter efficient. The open question is how a shared prompt can keep the knowledge of old classes while being updated for new ones. Existing methods protect old knowledge in parameter space, where they freeze task-specific prompts, expand new components, or sparsely gate which tokens receive gradients. However, this method sacrifices plasticity, yet the drift it still permits in shared tokens can corrupt the prompt's function on past classes. What must be preserved is the prompt's behavior. Anchoring the attention value allows the parameters to continue updating freely, as long as their interaction remains unchanged. TEA-Prompt achieves state-of-the-art accuracy with markedly lower forgetting on CIFAR-100, ImageNet-R, and CUB-200 with only 0.38M parameters. Code is publicy available at this https URL

  • Abstract: Low-precision pretraining is usually judged by validation loss. We ask whether matching loss also means learning rare facts as efficiently. Title: Mitigating Agentic Deception via Evaluation-Side Partially Observable Games

  • Authors: Hanxiao Li, Shutong Liu, Yongjia Ma, Jiaming Ji, Juntao Dai, Shuning Zhang, Kejian Shi, Kai Chen, Pu Feng, Yuqing Ma, Qi Dou, Yaodong Yang, Xianglong Liu, Simin Li

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: As large language models become more capable agents, they can exhibit deceptive behaviors, cheating human for higher rewards while appearing helpful. We attribute this failure to evaluation-side partial observability, where models are evaluated mainly from final outcome, without checking the faithfulness of underlying reasoning, tool use, and environmental effects. Prior mitigations address this partial observability by predefined evaluator latent belief space or one-shot monitoring, which do not extend to free-form language space and vulnerable to stronger deceptions that evade simple monitoring. In this work, we formulate deception mitigation as an Evaluation-Side Partially Observable Extensive Form Game (e-PoG), a max-min framework in which an agent maximizes reward, a monitor minimizes the agent's payoff by exposing deception from the full interaction trajectory, and a fixed verifier validates the monitor's claims and converts them into penalties. We define the solution concept as a Subgame-Perfect Oversight Equilibrium (SPOE) between agent and monitor, and prove its existence in finite games. With a perfect verifier, every SPOE is faithful; with an imperfect verifier, the guarantee persists if verifier errors do not reverse the ranking of monitor actions. To achieve faithful SPOE, we derive a two-timescale policy gradient algorithm and enhance the pipeline empirically with NLI verification and deterministic checks. Experiments with Gemma3-4B-IT and Qwen3-4B/8B on Agentic Role-Playing, SearchQA, and WebShop show that e-PoG mitigates multiple emergent deception modes, outperforming the strongest baselines by 26.0% in deception mitigation and by up to 39.8% in task success. These gains persist under weak-to-strong supervision. Code is available at this https URL.

  • Abstract: Latent Diffusion Models dominate generative modeling but face two-stage training complexity and the inherent reconstruction limits of pre-trained autoencoders. Pixel-space Flow Matching offers a simplified end-to-end alternative yet often encounters training difficulties when using the standard $v$-prediction objective. Recent research attributes this difficulty to the off-manifold nature of noised targets, motivating a shift toward $x$-prediction. In this paper, we investigate this explanation through patch-wise Principal Component Analysis (PCA) and identify a bandwidth bottleneck. The velocity in minor components is largely determined by the raw noisy input, requiring deep backbones to preserve information that competes with principal-component modeling. Through controlled experiments and representation analysis, we further examine why $x$-prediction remains effective: its parameterization provides a raw-input bypass, while its learned input representation can spontaneously concentrate on principal directions. Under low-rank observations, our analysis of the induced flow characterizes $x$-prediction as an implicit conditional-mean decoder, reconstructing discarded components from the retained information. These observations motivate the Raw-injected Transformer (RiT), which combines a lightweight raw-conditioned decoder with an output-side shortcut to separate semantic modeling from raw-state propagation. Our method restores effective $v$-prediction and achieves competitive performance on ImageNet $256\times256$, providing a perspective on the interaction between prediction objectives and architectural bandwidth.

Title: TUTTI: Parallel CKKS Bootstrapping in under 3 ms on CPU

  • Authors: Robin Köstler, Antonio Guimarães

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12854
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12854

  • Abstract: This paper introduces TUTTI, a new CKKS bootstrapping procedure that outperforms previous low-latency solutions in multi-threaded latency for any number of messages and, most notably, has a lower latency than TFHE when bootstrapping a single one. Differences between CKKS and lightweight schemes such as TFHE remain on several fronts, from functionality to key sizes and security assumptions, but our work shows that multi-threaded latency is no longer one of them, closing a long-standing gap in the practical FHE literature.

Title: WorkflowArena: Benchmarking Executable Workflow Orchestration in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems

  • Authors: Zheng Jiang, Fang Niu, Bo Peng, Houde Qian, Wenyi Li, Deyao Hong, Mingju Gao, Yizhe Chi, Youjie Zheng, Kaisen Yang, Calvin Xiao, Lifeng Sun, Huan-ang Gao

  • Abstract: Large language model agents are increasingly deployed as multi-agent systems rather than as a standalone model operating within a single context. The quality of this organization therefore constitutes a distinct capability, yet existing benchmarks either measure end-to-end task completion or evaluate coordination under structures specified in advance. We formalize this capability as workflow orchestration and introduce WorkflowArena, a benchmark of 145 long-horizon tasks spanning 8 professional domains, from software engineering and formal mathematics to legal analysis and 3D production. Title: Vendi Anomaly Scores for Efficient and Accurate Anomaly Detection

  • Authors: Amey Pasarkar, Adji Bousso Dieng
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12871
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12871
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12871]] Vendi Anomaly Scores for Efficient and Accurate Anomaly Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12871)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Anomaly detection (AD) is a critical for trustworthy machine learning and scientific discovery. Existing methods typically define anomalies through local density, isolation heuristics, or distance from a fitted distribution, making them sensitive to neighborhood scale, partitioning choices, or restrictive distributional assumptions. In this work, we introduce a new paradigm by formulating anomaly detection from a diversity perspective. We propose the $\textit{Vendi Anomaly Score}$ (VAS), which detects anomalies by quantifying how much a sample changes the diversity of the dataset, as measured by the Vendi score, when removed. VAS captures both local redundancy and global data structure without relying on density estimation or partitioning heuristics. Moreover, VAS requires no hyperparameter tuning, instead adapting automatically to the spectral structure of the data. VAS is non-parametric and scales linearly with dataset size. Across 148 benchmark embedding datasets derived from 10 source datasets and 4 embedding architectures, VAS achieves state-of-the-art AD performance. We further validate VAS on large-scale ImageNet experiments, where it remains robust as both contamination rate and dataset scale increase.

Title: Grasping the Future: Joint 3D Hand-Object Forecasting From Internet Videos

  • Authors: Rustin Soraki, Matthew Wallingford, Ryan Aby, Brandon Chee, Jason Ren, Ali Farhadi, Homanga Bharadhwaj, Roozbeh Mottaghi
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12879
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12879
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12879]] Grasping the Future: Joint 3D Hand-Object Forecasting From Internet Videos(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12879)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: To interact with an object, we need to anticipate both how it will move and how our hands should move with it. Human videos capture these relationships across everyday activities, offering a natural source for learning to predict future interactions. Learning from them requires recovering motion from completed interactions and using it to anticipate what could happen next. We present $\textit{Grasping-the-Future}$, a unified generative model of joint 3D hand-object motion, including wrist motion, finger articulation, and object position and orientation. Through masked spatiotemporal completion and flow matching, we train a single model for several prediction tasks: forecasting future interactions, predicting hand motion given object motion or vice versa, generating both trajectories toward a desired final object pose, and completing partially observed interactions. We explicitly model hand-object relations and incorporate contact constraints to help preserve grasp relationships during motion. To obtain 3D supervision from internet videos, we develop a scalable pipeline that combines pretrained perception models with joint geometric optimization to reconstruct completed interactions. We combine these recovered trajectories with annotated interaction datasets as training data for all tasks. We demonstrate strong performance across all tasks, outperforming released forecasters on datasets with 3D annotations. Moreover, we show geometric and physical plausibility of the predicted trajectories by retargeting them to a robotic embodiment. Videos are available at this https URL.

Title: Kolmogorov-Chapman Maps: Trajectory-Free Learning for Simulating Molecular Dynamics

  • Authors: Majdi Hassan, Marta Skreta, Dominique Beaini, Kirill Neklyudov
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12880
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12880
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12880]] Kolmogorov-Chapman Maps: Trajectory-Free Learning for Simulating Molecular Dynamics(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12880)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Stochastic differential equations (SDEs) describe stochastic processes across a wide range of scientific domains, including physics, biology, and finance. Although individual numerical integration steps are inexpensive, observing meaningful events or transitions can require millions of sequential steps, resulting in substantial wall-clock simulation time and poor hardware utilization. Deep learning offers an alternative by amortizing many integration steps into a single neural network forward pass, potentially enabling much faster simulation. However, existing supervised approaches typically require long simulated trajectory data, which restricts their applicability. In this work, we introduce Kolmogorov-Chapman Maps (KC-Maps), a method for learning the transition kernel of an SDE that relies only on the trajectory-free marginal samples together with the known drift and diffusion coefficients. Given an initial time and state of a process, KC-Maps models its distribution integrated to any given time, corresponding to the weak solution of the SDE. To recover the correct dynamics, KC-Maps satisfies two key properties: (i) it satisfies the boundary conditions, which we incorporate directly into the neural network architecture, and (ii) it respects the Kolmogorov--Chapman equation, which provides an efficient training objective. We empirically demonstrate that KC-Maps learns to integrate the dynamics of peptide molecular systems and outperforms classical molecular dynamics in terms of GPU-hours.

Title: Beyond Modality Preference: Controlling Multimodal Arbitration under Modality Conflict in MLLMs

  • Authors: Zhaoying Pan, Shenyu Lu, Jun Hui Wong, Junyi Chai, Xiaoqian Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12883
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12883
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12883]] Beyond Modality Preference: Controlling Multimodal Arbitration under Modality Conflict in MLLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12883)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) integrate information across modalities, but become unreliable when those modalities disagree. Under conflicting inputs, MLLMs may favor modalities that are not the most reliable for the task, thus motivating inference-time control of modality arbitration. We identify a small set of attention heads, termed arbitration heads, that causally influence whether predictions follow visual or textual evidence. Building on this, we introduce a lightweight, training-free intervention that biases these heads toward a designated trusted modality at inference time. The identified heads are stable across calibration set sizes and generalize across settings, and steering them enables effective bidirectional control of modality reliance across diverse benchmarks. Our findings identify an effective control mechanism to align MLLM predictions with task-specified modality priorities. The code is available at this https URL.

Title: Guiding Data Allocation for Robust Subpopulation Generalization

  • Authors: Zhaoying Pan, Yipei Wang, Shenyu Lu, Xiaoqian Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12884
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12884
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.12884]] Guiding Data Allocation for Robust Subpopulation Generalization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12884)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Machine learning models often achieve high average accuracy in the training data while performing poorly on test data due to subpopulation shift. A common data-centric solution is to enforce balanced subgroup proportions, but full balance can be costly, infeasible, and not always necessary. In this work, we study whether balanced training data is the unique optimal configuration for robust subpopulation generalization. We extend a Gaussian feature-model framework to theoretically characterize the performance landscape across general training subgroup distributions. We show that multiple configurations, including substantially imbalanced ones, can perform comparably to the balanced one. We further derive gradient-based directions for adjusting subgroup proportions to improve adjusted accuracy and worst-group accuracy more efficiently. These findings suggest that strategically allocating data offers a more flexible and principled path to robust performance than simple balancing. The code is available at this https URL.
